MolRSI identifies compounds with potent in vitro activity against MRSA. We next evaluated whether th<sub>e screen</sub>i<sub>ng wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>ope</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> M<sub>ol</sub>RSI <sub>can</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tif<sub>y an</sub>tib<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>compoun</sub>d<sub>s w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> b<sub>a</sub>l<sub>anc</sub>i<sub>ng ac</sub>ti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y,</sub> c<sub>y</sub>toto<sub>x</sub>icit<sub>y</sub> and <sub>s</sub>tr<sub>u</sub>ct<sub>u</sub>ral no<sub>v</sub>elt<sub>y.</sub> We <sub>us</sub>ed the <sub>s</sub>ame trainin<sub>g</sub> data<sub>s</sub>et<sub>s</sub> and <sub>s</sub>creenin<sub>g</sub> librar<sub>y</sub> of a<sub>pp</sub>ro<sub>x</sub>imatel<sub>y</sub> 12.1 million com<sub>p</sub>ounds as in our <sub>p</sub>revious ex<sub>p</sub>ert-driven stud<sub>y</sub> [1]. The workflow inte<sub>g</sub>rates o<sub>p</sub>timized antibacterial acti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y</sub> and c<sub>y</sub>toto<sub>x</sub>icit<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redictor<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>s</sub>tr<sub>u</sub>ct<sub>u</sub>ral filterin<sub>g</sub> and di<sub>v</sub>er<sub>s</sub>it<sub>y-</sub>ba<sub>s</sub>ed <sub>s</sub>election<sub>.</sub> Str<sub>u</sub>ct<sub>u</sub>ral filters excl<sub>u</sub>de s<sub>p</sub>ecified antibiotic cores and com<sub>p</sub>o<sub>u</sub>nds similar to kno<sub>w</sub>n antibiotics<sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hile c<sub>y</sub>totoxicit<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redi<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> f<sub>or</sub> ${ \mathrm { H e p G } } 2 ,$ HSkMC <sub>an</sub>d IMR<sub>-90</sub> <sub>ce</sub>ll<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orm</sub> <sub>can</sub>did<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>or</sub>iti<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> A<sub>n</sub>tib<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>ran</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> followed b<sub>y</sub> selection across structural clusters <sub>y</sub>ields 10<sub>7</sub> com<sub>p</sub>ounds for ex<sub>p</sub>erimental testin<sub>g</sub> (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure <sub>3</sub>a<sub>;</sub> M<sub>e</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s;</sub> S<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $\mathrm { S } _ { 3 \cdot 4 } )$

Ex<sub>p</sub>erimental validation identifies 1<sub>7</sub> com<sub>p</sub>ounds with minimum inhibitor concentrations (MICs) of $3 2 \mu \mathrm { g } / \mathrm { m L }$ or lower a<sub>g</sub>ainst methicillin-susce<sub>p</sub>tible S. aureus (MSSA). The hits occu<sub>py</sub> multi<sub>p</sub>le re<sub>g</sub>ions of the chemical s<sub>p</sub>ace (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure <sub>3</sub>b). Amon<sub>g</sub> these hits<sub>,</sub> C<sub>3</sub> and C<sub>3</sub>2 also exhibit <sub>p</sub>otent activit<sub>y</sub> a<sub>g</sub>ainst methicillin-resistant S. aureus (MRSA)<sub>,</sub> with MICs of 1 and $8 \mu \mathrm { g } / \mathrm { m L }$ <sub>,</sub> re<sub>sp</sub>ecti<sub>v</sub>el<sub>y.</sub> C<sub>3</sub> and $\mathrm { C } _ { 3 2 }$ <sub>are</sub> <sub>ran</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>2</sub>8th <sub>an</sub>d <sub>5</sub>th b<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redicted antibacterial activit<sub>y</sub> amon<sub>g</sub> the tested com<sub>p</sub>ounds (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure <sub>3</sub>c). Both com<sub>p</sub>ounds inhibit MRSA at <sub>concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>cause cy</sub>t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>ox</sub>i<sub>c</sub>it<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n eac</sub>h <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> th<sub>ree</sub> t<sub>ypes o</sub>f h<sub>uman ce</sub>ll<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> h<sub>a</sub>lf<sub>-max</sub>i<sub>ma</sub>l <sub>cy</sub>t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>ox</sub>i<sub>c</sub> <sub>concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> $\mathrm { ( C C _ { 5 0 } ) }$ ran<sub>g</sub>e <sup>f</sup>rom $6 . 4$ to $1 7 . 5 \mu \mathrm { g } / \mathrm { m I }$ f<sub>or</sub> $\mathrm { C } _ { 3 }$ <sub>an</sub>d f<sub>rom 24.2</sub> t<sub>o</sub> $5 4 { \cdot } 7 \mu \mathrm { g } / \mathrm { m I }$ f<sub>or</sub> C<sub>32.</sub> These meas<sub>u</sub>rements establish a se<sub>p</sub>aration bet<sub>w</sub>een antibacterial <sub>p</sub>otenc<sub>y</sub> and mammalian c<sub>y</sub>totoxicit<sub>y</sub> <sub>u</sub>nder the tested conditions (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure <sub>3</sub>d<sub>,g;</sub> Section S<sub>3</sub>.6).

MolRSI improves experimental hit rate and antibacterial activity prediction over the previous expert-driven workflow. The 17 hits among 107 tested compounds correspond to an experimental hit rate of 1<sub>5</sub>.<sub>9</sub>%<sub>,</sub> com<sub>p</sub>ared with 8.<sub>7</sub>% (21 of 2<sub>4</sub>1 com<sub>p</sub>ounds) in the <sub>p</sub>revious ex<sub>p</sub>ert-driven stud<sub>y</sub> at the same MSSA MIC threshold [1]. Thus<sub>,</sub> usin<sub>g</sub> the same trainin<sub>g</sub> datasets and screenin<sub>g</sub> librar<sub>y,</sub> the workflow develo<sub>p</sub>ed b<sub>y</sub> MolRSI achieves an a<sub>pp</sub>roximatel<sub>y</sub> 82% relative increase in observed hit rate (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure <sub>3</sub>e<sub>;</sub> Section S<sub>3</sub>.6.<sub>3</sub>).

[2]. Pe<sub>p</sub>tide identities<sub>,</sub> dataset sizes and label definitions are re<sub>p</sub>orted in S<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $\mathrm { S } _ { 4 \cdot 1 }$ .

Ranking metrics. We evaluated candidate rankings using the area under the precision–recall curve (AUPRC), the area under the receiver o<sub>p</sub>eratin<sub>g</sub> characteristic curve (AUROC) and the Enrichment Factor (EF). EF meas<sub>u</sub>res the <sub>p</sub>ositi<sub>v</sub>e fraction amon<sub>g</sub> to<sub>p</sub>-ranked candidates relati<sub>v</sub>e to the <sub>p</sub>ositi<sub>v</sub>e fraction in the f<sub>u</sub>ll tar<sub>g</sub>et d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>.</sub> F<sub>or a</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith $N$ <sub>can</sub>did<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es an</sub>d $N _ { + }$ ex<sub>p</sub>erimentall<sub>y p</sub>ositi<sub>v</sub>e binders<sub>,</sub> let $n _ { f }$ b<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e num</sub>b<sub>er re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>op-ran</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $f$ <sub>an</sub>d $n _ { + , f }$ th<sub>e num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>ves among</sub> th<sub>em.</sub> W<sub>e ca</sub>l<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d

$$
\mathrm { E F } _ { f } = \frac { n _ { + , f } / n _ { f } } { N _ { + } / N } , \qquad f \in \{ 0 . 0 1 , 0 . 0 2 , 0 . 0 5 , 0 . 1 \} .\tag{2}
$$

Step 1: Selecting workflow families. Family selection combines the best available current-task development <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance, an exp</sub>l<sub>ora</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>onus an</sub>d <sub>a</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er score.</sub> Hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow scores are</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>di<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>en</sub> <sub>aggrega</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-s</sub>i<sub>m</sub>il<sub>ar</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>score en</sub>t<sub>ers se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on as an a</sub>dditi<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>erm.</sub> A <sub>correspon</sub>di<sub>ng wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>score en</sub>t<sub>ers paren</sub>t <sub>samp</sub>li<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub> St<sub>ep</sub> 2 <sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>p</sub>li<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>or.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>use</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>t<sub>eps</sub> <sub>are</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d t<sub>oge</sub>th<sub>er</sub> b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ow.</sub>

Historical score normalization. Let $x _ { \tau } ( v )$ denote the develo<sub>p</sub>ment score of workflow v on source task $\tau ,$ <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathcal { V } _ { \tau }$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n comp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ows w</sub>ith fi<sub>n</sub>it<sub>e</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>opmen</sub>t <sub>scores on</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>.</sub> S<sub>cores are</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>or</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>so</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t l<sub>arger va</sub>l<sub>ues</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er per</sub>f<sub>ormance:</sub>

$$
s _ { \tau } ( v ) = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { x _ { \tau } ( v ) , } & { { \mathrm { f o r ~ a ~ h i g h e r - i s \mathrm { - } b e t t e r ~ m e t r i c } } , } \\ { - x _ { \tau } ( v ) , } & { { \mathrm { f o r ~ a ~ l o w e r - i s \mathrm { - } b e t t e r ~ m e t r i c } } . } \end{array} \right. }\tag{<sub>3</sub>}
$$

E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>score</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>en norma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e o</sub>th<sub>er comp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ows on</sub> th<sub>e same source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>:</sub>

$$
\widetilde s _ { \tau } ( v ) = \frac { s _ { \tau } ( v ) - \mathrm { m e d i a n } _ { u \in \mathcal { V } _ { \tau } } s _ { \tau } ( u ) } { \operatorname* { m a x } \{ \mathrm { M A D } _ { u \in \mathcal { V } _ { \tau } } ( s _ { \tau } ( u ) ) , \epsilon \} } .\tag{<sub>4</sub>}
$$

H<sub>ere,</sub> $\mathrm { M A D } ( s ) = \mathrm { m e d i a n } \left| s - \mathrm { m e d i a n } ( s ) \right|$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e me</sub>di<sub>an a</sub>b<sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e me</sub>di<sub>an, an</sub>d $\epsilon > 0$ <sub>p</sub>re<sub>v</sub>ents di<sub>v</sub>ision b<sub>y</sub> zero<sub>.</sub> The normalized score is ma<sub>pp</sub>ed to a bo<sub>u</sub>nded <sub>u</sub>tilit<sub>y:</sub>

$$
\bar { s } _ { \tau } ( v ) = 2 \sigma ( \widetilde s _ { \tau } ( v ) ) - 1 , \qquad \sigma ( x ) = { \frac { 1 } { 1 + e ^ { - x } } } .\tag{<sub>5</sub>}
$$

The re<sub>su</sub>ltin<sub>g u</sub>tilit<sub>y</sub> lie<sub>s</sub> in $[ - 1 , 1 ]$ <sub>, a</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>comes measure</sub>d <sub>on</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e aggrega</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>an un</sub>b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>rom a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>.</sub> All <sub>scores use</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> thi<sub>s ca</sub>l<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on were ava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>opmen</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e source wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow;</sub> h<sub>e</sub>ld<sub>-ou</sub>t t<sub>es</sub>t <sub>scores are exc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

Task-similarity weights. For a target task $\tau _ { 0 } ,$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k $\tau$ i<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>ei<sub>g</sub>hted accordin<sub>g</sub> to e<sub>v</sub>al<sub>u</sub>ation<sub>-</sub>metric com<sub>p</sub>atibilit<sub>y,</sub> ta<sub>s</sub>k t<sub>yp</sub>e<sub>,</sub> data<sub>s</sub>et <sub>s</sub>i<sub>z</sub>e and ta<sub>s</sub>k<sub>-</sub>de<sub>s</sub>cri<sub>p</sub>tion <sub>s</sub>imilarit<sub>y:</sub>

$$
w ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) = w _ { \mathrm { m e t r i c } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) w _ { \mathrm { t y p e } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) w _ { \mathrm { s i z e } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) w _ { \mathrm { e m b } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) .\tag{6}
$$

Th<sub>e non-nega</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> $w _ { \mathrm { m e t r i c } }$ <sub>an</sub>d $w _ { \mathrm { t y p e } }$ encode com<sub>p</sub>atibilit<sub>y</sub> of e<sub>v</sub>al<sub>u</sub>ation metrics and task t<sub>yp</sub>es<sub>.</sub> Dataset-<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze s</sub>imil<sub>a</sub>rit<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s ca</sub>l<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub>

$$
w _ { \mathrm { s i z e } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) = \exp [ - \gamma \left| \log \left| \mathfrak { D } _ { \tau } \right| - \log \left| \mathfrak { D } _ { \tau _ { 0 } } \right| \right| ] ,\tag{<sub>7</sub>}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $| \mathcal { D } _ { \tau } |$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze assoc</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith t<sub>as</sub>k $\tau ,$ <sub>, an</sub>d $\gamma > 0$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e pena</sub>lt<sub>y</sub> f<sub>or s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> dif<sub>erences.</sub> De<sub>s</sub>cri<sub>p</sub>tion <sub>s</sub>imilarit<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s</sub> calc<sub>u</sub>lated from ta<sub>s</sub>k<sub>-</sub>de<sub>s</sub>cri<sub>p</sub>tion embeddin<sub>gs</sub> $\mathbf { e } _ { \tau }$ <sub>an</sub>d $\mathbf { e } _ { \tau _ { 0 } }$ :

$$
w _ { \mathrm { e m b } } ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) = \mathrm { m a x } \{ \mathrm { c o s } ( { \bf e } _ { \tau } , { \bf e } _ { \tau _ { 0 } } ) , 0 \} .\tag{8}
$$

Family and workflow transfer scores. Let $\mathcal { T } _ { f }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng an eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y</sub> $f ,$ <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathcal { V } _ { \tau , f }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ows</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y on source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k $\tau .$ Th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il <sub>-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er score</sub> i<sub>s</sub>

$$

Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>ca</sub>l<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>averages</sub> <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance</sub> <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>en</sub> <sub>com</sub>bi<sub>nes</sub> th<sub>ose</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>averages.</sub> F<sub>or a</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>-</sub>t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> $v _ { i } ,$ l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathcal { T } _ { i }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng an e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>.</sub> The matchin<sub>g</sub> rule uses workflow famil<sub>y</sub> and modification t<sub>yp</sub>e, and matched $( v _ { i } , \tau )$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on source</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k $\tau .$ Th<sub>e wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er score</sub> i<sub>s</sub>

$$
\mathrm { t r a n s f e r } ( v _ { i } ) = \frac { \displaystyle \sum _ { \tau \in \mathcal { T } _ { i } } w ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) \bar { s } _ { \tau } ( \mathrm { m a t c h e d } ( v _ { i } , \tau ) ) } { \displaystyle \sum _ { \tau \in \mathcal { T } _ { i } } w ( \tau , \tau _ { 0 } ) } .\tag{10}

T<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>an e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e recor</sub>d <sub>are exc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e correspon</sub>di<sub>ng numera</sub>t<sub>or an</sub>d d<sub>enom</sub>i<sub>na</sub>t<sub>or.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>e</sub>d <sub>averages are</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht i<sub>s pos</sub>iti<sub>ve.</sub> With <sub>non-nega</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s an</sub>d utilities in [−1, 1], each defined transfer score also lies in [−1, 1].

Family-selection rule. Let b index family-selection decisions, $\mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { e l i g i b l e } }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> f<sub>am</sub>ili<sub>es</sub> <sub>an</sub>d $n _ { b } ( f ) \geq 1$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>it <sub>coun</sub>t f<sub>or</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y</sub> $f .$ Th<sub>e nex</sub>t f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>accor</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>o</sub>

$$
f _ { b } = \underset { f \in \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { e l i g i b l e } } } { \arg \operatorname* { m a x } } \left[ \underset { \mathrm { c u r r e n t - t a s k } } { \underbrace { \exp \mathrm { l o i t } _ { b } ( f ) } } + \underset { \mathrm { e x p l o r a t i o n } } { \underbrace { \alpha \sqrt { \frac { \ln ( b + 1 ) } { n _ { b } ( f ) } } } } + \underset { \mathrm { t r a n s f e r } } { \underbrace { \mathrm { t r a n s f e r } ( f ) } } \right] .\tag{11}
$$

Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>o</sub>it<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>erm</sub> <sub>summar</sub>i<sub>zes</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>es</sub>t <sub>curren</sub>t<sub>-</sub>t<sub>as</sub>k d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>opmen</sub>t <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance</sub> <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y</sub> $f ,$ incl<sub>u</sub>din<sub>g</sub> stability and overfitting adjustments, on a scale from zero to one. The coeficient α controls exploration, with lar<sub>g</sub>er bonuses for less-visited families [2]. Step 2: Selecting parent workflows. Let $\mathcal { V }$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>comp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>,</sub> <sub>non-ensem</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>wor</sub>kfl<sub>ows</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith fi<sub>n</sub>it<sub>e</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>opmen</sub>t <sub>scores on</sub> th<sub>e curren</sub>t t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>.</sub> F<sub>or wor</sub>kfl<sub>ow</sub> $v _ { i } \in \mathcal V$ <sub>,</sub> l<sub>e</sub>t $q _ { i }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>curren</sub>t<sub>-</sub>t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>score</sub> <sub>supp</sub>li<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e samp</sub>l<sub>er an</sub>d $c _ { i }$ th<sub>e num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f <sub>ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng c</sub>hild<sub>ren.</sub> S<sub>amp</sub>li<sub>ng we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>pro</sub>b<sub>a</sub>biliti<sub>es are</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { w _ { i } = \sigma \left( \displaystyle { \beta \frac { q _ { i } - \mathrm { m e d i a n } _ { v _ { j } \in \mathcal { V } } q _ { j } } { \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ \mathrm { M A D } _ { v _ { j } \in \mathcal { V } } ( q _ { j } ) , \epsilon \right\} } } \right) \frac { 1 } { 1 + c _ { i } } \left[ 1 + \lambda \mathrm { t r a n s f e r } ( v _ { i } ) \right] , } \\ & { p ( v _ { i } ) = \displaystyle { \frac { w _ { i } } { \sum _ { v _ { j } \in \mathcal { V } } w _ { j } } } . } \end{array}\tag{12}
$$

Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t $\beta$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l<sub>s</sub> <sub>sens</sub>iti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>di<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>curren</sub>t<sub>-</sub>t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>scores.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>or</sub> $1 / ( 1 + c _ { i } )$ <sub>re</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> re<sub>p</sub>eated ex<sub>p</sub>ansion of the same <sub>p</sub>arent, and λ controls the contribution of historical transfer. ## Complete antibacterial task prompt: Claude Code, E. coli

You are participating in the antibiotic activity benchmark. Your job is to find the best   
model you can for one task, under a fixed protocol.   
BENCHMARK INSTRUCTIONS   
- The task-specific description is provided below. Follow it exactly.   
Work in ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/claude\_code/activity\_E\_coli‘. The dataset is at ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/   
claude\_code/activity\_E\_coli/dataset\_activity\_E\_coli/‘.   
The fixed helper ‘prepare.py‘ is at ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/claude\_code/activity\_E\_coli/prepare.py‘.   
Import and use it. Do not modify it:   
import sys; sys.path.insert(0, "<BASELINE\_ROOT>/claude\_code/activity\_E\_coli")   
from prepare import (load\_seed\_split, load\_seed\_test, evaluate,   
save\_predictions, record\_step, SEEDS, METRIC,   
budget\_remaining)   
load\_seed\_split(seed) -> (train\_df, val\_df) both carry Y   
load\_seed\_test(seed) -> test\_df carries Y, for scoring only   
evaluate(y\_true, y\_pred) -> float uses this task’s metric   
save\_predictions(step, seed, test\_df, y\_pred) -> submission/step\_NN/   
record\_step(step, val\_scores, description) closes one candidate   
budget\_remaining() -> int   
You must train and evaluate on all five fixed seed splits ‘1, 2, 3, 4, 5‘.

For every candidate, number it (1, 2, 3, ...), call ‘save\_predictions(step, seed, ...)‘ for each of the five seeds, then call ‘record\_step(step, val\_scores, description)‘ once. Nothing is overwritten between steps, so every candidate stays scoreable afterwards.

\- For each seed print exactly: [seed N] val\_auprc=VALUE

\- Your final message must contain the result line in the exact format:

‘[result] AUPRC = MEAN +/- STD‘

reporting the VALIDATION mean and standard deviation of your selected candidate, and must state which step number you selected.

\- The mean validation metric across the 5 seed splits is the sole optimization and model-selection target.

\- ‘test.csv‘ carries ‘Y‘ so that predictions can be scored offline after this run. Never read it, train on it, or compute a test score during the search — model selection is validation-only.

\- Do not modify dataset files.

## ADDITIONAL NOTES

\- Compute: NVIDIA L40S GPU and 20 CPU threads. ‘prepare.py‘ pins both on import; do not override them.

\- Host folder access: if you need it, call ‘request\_host\_access‘ for exactly ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/claude\_code‘

with mode ‘rw‘. That one grant covers this task’s dataset, prepare.py and submission directory, so do not request any narrower or additional path.

\- Total runtime: there is no hard wall-clock limit. Your budget is the experiment count below, so spend it on better ideas rather than rushing.

\- Total steps: at most 20 candidate experiments. This is enforced, not advisory: ‘record\_step‘ raises once the 20th is recorded. Call ‘budget\_remaining()‘ to check, and plan so your best configuration is measured before the budget runs out.

\- No starting method is prescribed. Deciding what to try is part of the task.

\- Live web search is available in this Claude Code session. You may search online for the models and methods that actually work for this kind of endpoint — recent molecular representation models, pretrained embeddings, domain-specific architectures — before committing budget to a candidate. You may install and use any package or published method you judge promising. Searching and installing do not consume experiment budget; only a recorded candidate does. This is optional, not required.

\- Use the budget to iterate and improve, but keep each candidate reasonably efficient.

Work one candidate at a time. Do not plan or fix the whole sequence in advance: after each ‘record\_step‘, read that candidate’s five validation scores and let what you just learned decide the next candidate. Each candidate should be a focused change motivated by the results so far, not the next item on a pre-made list.

## TASK DESCRIPTION

\# Molecular Property Prediction Benchmark — activity\_E\_coli

## ## Task

Predict the molecular property endpoint \*\*activity\_E\_coli\*\* from molecular SMILES strings.

\- Dataset id: ‘antibiotic\_\_activity\_E\_coli‘

\- Dataset family: antibiotic

\- Category: activity

\- Task type: classification

\- Primary metric: \*\*AUPRC\*\* (higher is better)

\- Train/validation pool: 35340-35341 molecules depending on seed

\- Held-out test set: 3926-3927 molecules depending on seed; you must produce per-seed test

predictions for every candidate   
Split: {"fingerprint": {"nbits": 2048, "radius": 2, "type": "Morgan"}, "method": "   
greedy\_leader\_tanimoto", "similarity\_threshold": 0.4, "stratified\_by": "Y", "test\_fraction":   
0.1, "val\_fraction": 0.1}   
## Data   
The uploaded ‘dataset\_activity\_E\_coli/‘ directory belongs only to \*\*activity\_E\_coli\*\*. It contains   
‘meta.json‘ and five fixed split directories, ‘seed\_1/‘ through ‘seed\_5/‘. Before running any   
experiment, verify that ‘dataset\_activity\_E\_coli/meta.json‘ has ‘name‘ equal to ‘   
activity\_E\_coli‘, ‘dataset\_id‘ equal to ‘antibiotic\_\_activity\_E\_coli‘, and primary metric   
equal to ‘AUPRC‘; stop and report a dataset mismatch if any value differs. Each seed directory   
contains ‘train.csv‘, ‘val.csv‘, and ‘test.csv‘. The labeled training and validation files   
contain ‘Drug\_ID‘, ‘Drug‘ (SMILES), ‘Y‘ (label/target), and may contain precomputed molecule  
derived descriptor columns. The test files contain the same columns, including ‘Y‘; ‘Y‘ is   
present only so that predictions can be aligned and scored afterwards, and must never be read   
during training, tuning, or model selection.   
Use the fixed split files directly:   
‘‘‘python   
import pandas as pd   
SEEDS = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]   
for seed in SEEDS:   
train\_df = pd.read\_csv(f"dataset\_activity\_E\_coli/seed\_{seed}/train.csv")   
val\_df = pd.read\_csv(f"dataset\_activity\_E\_coli/seed\_{seed}/val.csv")   
666   
Model selection is validation-only: never let test labels or test performance influence training,   
tuning, search, or candidate choice. For every candidate you evaluate, do generate and save   
per-seed test predictions via ‘prepare.save\_predictions‘ — they are scored afterwards, outside   
this run. Do not create alternative train/validation splits and do not merge train and   
validation during search.   
## Evaluation   
The primary validation metric is \*\*AUPRC\*\* (higher is better). Evaluate each candidate   
independently on all five fixed seed splits. Compute the metric separately for each seed, then   
report the arithmetic mean and sample standard deviation (‘ddof=1‘) across the five values.   
Use ‘roc\_auc\_score‘ for AUROC, ‘average\_precision\_score‘ for AUPRC, ‘mean\_absolute\_error‘ for   
MAE, and ‘spearmanr(...).statistic‘ for Spearman. Classification metrics must be computed from   
positive-class probabilities, not hard class labels.   
For every candidate, print exactly one validation line for each seed followed by the aggregate   
result:   
‘‘‘text   
[seed 1] val\_auprc=VALUE   
[seed 2] val\_auprc=VALUE   
[seed 3] val\_auprc=VALUE   
[seed 4] val\_auprc=VALUE   
[seed 5] val\_auprc=VALUE   
[result] AUPRC = MEAN +/- STD

Complete pMHC ranking task prompt: Codex, pmhc   
You are participating in the protein binder ranking benchmark. Your job is to find the best   
ranking workflow you can for one task, under a fixed protocol.   
BENCHMARK INSTRUCTIONS   
- The task-specific description is provided below. Follow it exactly.   
- Work in ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/codex/pmhc\_binder\_ranking‘. The dataset is at ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/codex/   
pmhc\_binder\_ranking/dataset\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking/‘.   
- The fixed helper ‘prepare.py‘ is at ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/codex/pmhc\_binder\_ranking/prepare.py‘.   
Import and use it. Do not modify it:   
import sys; sys.path.insert(0, "<BASELINE\_ROOT>/codex/pmhc\_binder\_ranking")   
from prepare import (load\_search, load\_test, evaluate,   
save\_predictions, record\_step, SEARCH, METRIC,   
budget\_remaining)   
load\_search(collection) -> df carries Y   
load\_test() -> df carries Y, for scoring only   
evaluate(y\_true, y\_score) -> float uses this task’s metric   
save\_predictions(step, collection, df, y\_score) -> submission/step\_NN/<collection>.csv   
with design\_id and score   
record\_step(step, search\_scores, description) closes one candidate   
budget\_remaining() -> int   
- You must score every candidate on both search collections in ‘SEARCH‘.   
- For every candidate, number it (1, 2, 3, ...), call ‘save\_predictions(step, collection, ...)‘   
for both search collections and for the held-out test collection, then call   
‘record\_step(step, search\_scores, description)‘ once. Nothing is overwritten between

steps, so every candidate stays scoreable afterwards.

\- For each search collection print exactly: [COLLECTION] val\_auroc=VALUE

\- Your final message must contain the result line in the exact format:

‘[result] AUROC = MEAN +/- STD‘

reporting the SEARCH mean and standard deviation of your selected candidate, and must state which step number you selected.

\- The mean search AUROC over the two gp100 collections is the sole optimization and model-selection target.

The test collection carries ‘Y‘ so that predictions can be scored offline after this run. Never read it, train on it, or compute a test score during the search — workflow selection uses the search collections only.

\- Do not modify dataset files.

## ADDITIONAL NOTES

\- Compute: 300 A100 GPU-hours. Jobs are single-GPU and independent; pin ‘CUDA\_VISIBLE\_DEVICES‘ on every one of them, because several folding stacks open every visible GPU on import and one unpinned job destroys throughput on its whole host.

\- Folded structures and model scores may be cached and reused across candidates. Label-free caching of this kind does not consume an experiment, so fold once and score many times rather than refolding per candidate.

Workspace: ‘<BASELINE\_ROOT>/codex/pmhc\_binder\_ranking‘ is writable and network access is enabled, so you can install packages and fetch model weights. Write nothing outside that directory, and treat the dataset directory inside it as read-only.

\- Total runtime: there is no hard wall-clock limit. Your budget is the experiment count below and the GPU-hours above, so spend them on better ideas rather than rushing.

\- Total steps: at most 20 candidate experiments. This is enforced, not advisory:

‘record\_step‘ raises once the 20th is recorded. Call ‘budget\_remaining()‘ to check, and plan so your best workflow is measured before the budget runs out.

\- No starting workflow is prescribed. Deciding what to try is part of the task.

\- Web search is available in this session. You may search online for the

models and methods that actually work for this kind of endpoint — structure predictors, interface-confidence metrics, published ranking criteria, pMHC-specific scoring — before committing budget to a candidate. You may install and use any package or published method you judge promising. Searching and installing do not consume experiment budget; only a recorded candidate does. This is optional, not required.

\- Use the budget to iterate and improve, but keep each candidate reasonably efficient.

Work one candidate at a time. Do not plan or fix the whole sequence in advance: after each ‘record\_step‘, read that candidate’s search scores and let what you just learned decide the next candidate. Each candidate should be a focused change motivated by the results so far, not the next item on a pre-made list.

## TASK DESCRIPTION

\# Protein Binder Ranking Benchmark — pmhc\_binder\_ranking

## ## Task

Rank designed miniprotein binders against peptide-MHC class I complexes: assign a scalar score to each target--binder complex, higher for candidates that were selected in the screen.

\- Dataset id: ‘protein\_\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking‘

\- Dataset family: protein binder ranking

\- Category: pMHC

\- Task type: classification (binary selection outcome)

\- Primary metric: \*\*AUROC\*\* (higher is better)

Search collections: two gp100 HLA-A2 complexes, 2,611 and 3,271 candidates   
Held-out test collection: MART-1 (A2L; ELAGIGILTV), 3,845 candidates; you must produce test   
predictions for every candidate   
Partition: fixed and given -- both gp100 collections are the search set, MART-1 is the test set   
## Data   
The uploaded ‘dataset\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking/‘ directory belongs only to \*\*pmhc\_binder\_ranking\*\*. It   
contains ‘meta.json‘ and three collection directories: ‘gp100\_YLEPGPVTA/‘ and ‘gp100\_IMDQVPFSV   
/‘, which are the search collections, and ‘mart1\_ELAGIGILTV/‘, which is the test collection.   
Before running any experiment, verify that ‘dataset\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking/meta.json‘ has ‘name‘   
equal to ‘pmhc\_binder\_ranking‘, ‘dataset\_id‘ equal to ‘protein\_\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking‘ and   
primary metric equal to ‘AUROC‘, and that the two search collections hold 2,611 candidates   
with 14 positives and 3,271 candidates with 4 positives respectively; stop and report a   
dataset mismatch if any value differs.   
Each collection directory contains ‘candidates.csv‘ and the target specification it was screened   
against: ‘target.yaml‘, carrying the ‘hla\_allele‘, ‘hla\_sequence‘, ‘b2m\_sequence‘ and ‘peptide‘   
fields; ‘msa/hla.a3m‘ and ‘msa/b2m.a3m‘, the frozen target-chain alignments; and ‘target.cif‘,   
the reference complex structure for that pMHC. ‘candidates.csv‘ carries ‘design\_id‘,   
binder\_seq‘ (102 aa, 20 canonical residues) and ‘Y‘, the binary outcome of the sorting   
experiment, where ‘1‘ means the candidate was selected. The test collection has the same   
columns, including ‘Y‘; ‘Y‘ is present only so that predictions can be aligned and scored   
afterwards, and must never be read during workflow development, tuning or selection.   
Use the fixed collections directly:   
‘‘‘python   
import pandas as pd   
SEARCH = ["gp100\_YLEPGPVTA", "gp100\_IMDQVPFSV"]   
for collection in SEARCH:   
df = pd.read\_csv(f"dataset\_pmhc\_binder\_ranking/{collection}/candidates.csv")   
666   
Workflow selection is search-only: never let test labels or test performance influence scoring,   
tuning, search or candidate choice. For every candidate you evaluate, do generate and save   
test predictions via ‘prepare.save\_predictions‘ -- they are scored afterwards, outside this   
run. Do not create alternative partitions, and do not merge the search and test collections   
during search.   
Three properties of this data decide what a result here means. Positives are scarce: 18 across the   
two search collections, so a handful of candidates moves the metric and a difference of a few   
points of AUROC is not a reliable ordering of two workflows. The two search collections   
present different gp100 peptides on the same HLA-A2 scaffold and differ only in the peptide   
chain, while the test collection presents an unrelated peptide, so a workflow fitted to gp100-   
specific features will not transfer. The negatives are hard by construction: every candidate   
in every collection was a design that a generative pipeline already ranked highly enough to be   
worth ordering, so separation here will not reproduce on a designed-versus-random task.   
## Evaluation   
The primary metric is \*\*AUROC\*\* (higher is better). Evaluate each candidate independently on both   
search collections. Compute the metric separately for each collection, then report the   
arithmetic mean and sample standard deviation (‘ddof=1‘) across the two values. Use ‘

roc\_auc\_score‘ for AUROC and ‘average\_precision\_score‘ for AUPRC, and compute both from   
continuous scores, not hard class labels. AUPRC and enrichment factor may be recorded as   
diagnostics, but the search mean AUROC is the selection target.   
For every candidate, print exactly one validation line for each search collection followed by the   
aggregate result:   
‘‘‘text   
[gp100\_YLEPGPVTA] val\_auroc=VALUE   
[gp100\_IMDQVPFSV] val\_auroc=VALUE   
[result] AUROC = MEAN +/- STD   
666   
Both search lines and the final result line are mandatory. The search mean AUROC is the sole   
optimization and model-selection target.   
## Experiment Budget   
Run at most 20 complete candidate experiments, named ‘step\_01‘ through ‘step\_20‘. One budget unit   
is one candidate ranking workflow evaluated on both search collections. Evaluate one candidate   
at a time. A candidate whose evaluation starts but crashes or times out still consumes its   
numbered step; repair attempts on the same candidate do not consume an additional step.   
Environment inspection and deterministic label-independent caching of folded structures and   
model scores do not consume a step, but unlogged pilot experiments, hidden parameter sweeps   
and extra candidate evaluations are not allowed.   
After each step, record the candidate description, complete source code, command, stdout/stderr,   
runtime, GPU-hours used, status, both search values, the search mean and the sample standard   
deviation. Preserve every step’s artifacts without overwriting earlier steps. After the final   
step, select the completed candidate with the best search mean AUROC and preserve its source   
code as the final solution.   
## Constraints   
- Do not modify the dataset files, labels, fixed partition, primary metric or evaluation   
definition.   
- Do not use test labels, test predictions or test performance for scoring, tuning, search or   
workflow selection.   
- Keep the partition intact; every candidate must be scored on both search collections and must   
produce test predictions.   
- Do not exceed 300 A100 GPU-hours, and pin ‘CUDA\_VISIBLE\_DEVICES‘ on every job.   
- Do not add extra search steps.   
## Final Response   
At the end, report the selected step, its two per-collection search AUROC values, the search mean   
+/- sample standard deviation, the number of completed steps out of 20, failed steps, the GPU  
hours used out of 300, and the paths to the selected source code and complete step logs.

Prospective design workflow. ## S3.2 Evaluation of recursive self-improvement

Source experience. MolRSI developed workflows independently on each of the 25 source tasks and pooled the re<sub>su</sub>ltin<sub>g</sub> e<sub>xp</sub>erience in <sub>s</sub>ol<sub>u</sub>tion<sub>,</sub> refinement and e<sub>x</sub>ec<sub>u</sub>tion memor<sub>y.</sub> Each antibacterial tar<sub>g</sub>et be<sub>g</sub>an <sub>w</sub>ith the same <sub>p</sub>ooled so<sub>u</sub>rce ex<sub>p</sub>erience<sub>.</sub> Ex<sub>p</sub>erience <sub>g</sub>enerated d<sub>u</sub>rin<sub>g</sub> a tar<sub>g</sub>et search <sub>gu</sub>ided later iterations of that <sub>searc</sub>h <sub>an</sub>d <sub>was no</sub>t <sub>s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s.</sub>

Workflow diferences. The workflows difered in their predictive models, filter order and thresholds, and com<sub>p</sub>ound-selection <sub>p</sub>rocedures (Su<sub>pp</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> Fi<sub>g</sub>ure S2). ## S3.6 Experimental validation

## S3.6.1 Antibacterial activity assays

Bacterial strains and culture. S. aureus $\mathrm { R N } _ { 4 2 2 0 }$ (MSSA) was obtained from DSMZ (DSM $2 6 3 0 9 )$ <sub>an</sub>d S<sub>.</sub> aureus FPR<sub>3757</sub>/USA<sub>3</sub>00 (MRSA) from ATCC (BAA-1<sub>55</sub>6). Design targets. Each prospective design task was specified by an HLA allele, an on-target and of-target <sub>p</sub>e<sub>p</sub>tide <sub>p</sub>air<sub>,</sub> so<sub>u</sub>rce str<sub>u</sub>ct<sub>u</sub>res for se<sub>qu</sub>ence and hots<sub>p</sub>ot <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>v</sub>enance<sub>,</sub> chain assi<sub>g</sub>nments<sub>,</sub> a binder-len<sub>g</sub>th ran<sub>g</sub>e<sub>,</sub> and ex<sub>p</sub>osed <sub>p</sub>e<sub>p</sub>tide hots<sub>p</sub>ots (Su<sub>pp</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> Table S1<sub>4</sub>). Within each tar<sub>g</sub>et <sub>p</sub>air<sub>,</sub> the HLA heav<sub>y</sub>-chain and β -micro<sub>g</sub>lobulin se<sub>q</sub>uences were held fixed and onl<sub>y</sub> the <sub>p</sub>e<sub>p</sub>tide se<sub>q</sub>uence was chan<sub>g</sub>ed. The source PDB coordinates <sub>w</sub>ere not s<sub>upp</sub>lied as desi<sub>g</sub>n in<sub>pu</sub>ts<sub>.</sub> The<sub>y</sub> <sub>w</sub>ere <sub>u</sub>sed onl<sub>y</sub> to establish the tar<sub>g</sub>et-chain se<sub>qu</sub>ences and select <sub>p</sub>e<sub>p</sub>tide hots<sub>p</sub>ots.
